=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N islands and M bidirectional bridges connecting two islands. The islands and bridges are numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N and 1, 2, \ldots, M, respectively.
Bridge i connects islands U_i and V_i, and the time it takes to cross it in either direction is T_i.
No bridge connects an island to itself, but it is possible for two islands to be directly connected by more than one bridge.
One can travel between any two islands using some bridges.
You are given Q queries, so answer each of them. The i-th query is as follows:

You are given K_i distinct bridges: bridges B_{i,1}, B_{i,2}, \ldots, B_{i,K_i}.
Find the minimum time required to travel from island 1 to island N using each of these bridges at least once.
Only consider the time spent crossing bridges.
You can cross the given bridges in any order and in any direction.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
U_1 V_1 T_1
U_2 V_2 T_2
\vdots
U_M V_M T_M
Q
K_1
B_{1,1} B_{1,2} \cdots B_{1,{K_1}}
K_2
B_{2,1} B_{2,2} \cdots B_{2,{K_2}}
\vdots
K_Q
B_{Q,1} B_{Q,2} \cdots B_{Q,{K_Q}}

Output

Print Q lines. The i-th line (1 \leq i \leq Q) should contain the answer to the i-th query as an integer.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 400
- N-1 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq U_i < V_i \leq N
- 1 \leq T_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq Q \leq 3000
- 1 \leq K_i \leq 5
- 1 \leq B_{i,1} < B_{i,2} < \cdots < B_{i,K_i} \leq M
- All input values are integers.
- It is possible to travel between any two islands using some bridges.

Sample Input 1

3 5
1 2 10
1 3 20
1 3 30
2 3 15
2 3 25
2
1
1
2
3 5

Sample Output 1

25
70

For the first query, we need to find the minimum time to travel from island 1 to island 3 while using bridge 1.
The minimum time is achieved by using bridge 1 to move from island 1 to island 2, then using bridge 4 to move from island 2 to island 3. The time taken is 10 + 15 = 25.
Hence, print 25 on the first line.
For the second query, we need to find the minimum time to travel from island 1 to island 3 while using both bridges 3 and 5.
The minimum time is achieved by using bridge 3 to move from island 1 to island 3, then using bridge 5 to move to island 2, and finally using bridge 4 to return to island 3. The time taken is 30 + 25 + 15 = 70.
Hence, print 70 on the second line.

Sample Input 2

6 6
1 5 1
2 5 1
2 4 1
3 4 1
3 6 1
1 6 1
2
5
1 2 3 4 5
1
5

Sample Output 2

5
3

For each query, you can cross the specified bridges in either direction.

Sample Input 3

5 5
1 2 1000000000
2 3 1000000000
3 4 1000000000
4 5 1000000000
1 5 1000000000
1
1
3

Sample Output 3

4000000000

Beware that the answer may not fit in a 32-bit integer.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Per query enumeration:
from itertools import permutations
best = INF
for perm in permutations(range(K)):
    for mask in range(1<<K):
        cost = dist[s][start(perm[0], mask)]
        prev_end = end(perm[0], mask)
        for idx in range(1,K):
            item = perm[idx]
            cost += dist[prev_end][start(item,mask)]
            prev_end = end(item,mask)
        cost += dist[prev_end][t]
        if cost < best: best = cost
answer = base_sum + best

Pseudo:
def solve_query(edge_ids):
    K=len(edge_ids)
    starts = [[0]*2 for _ in range(K)]
    ends = [[0]*2 for _ in range(K)]
    base=0
    for idx,eid in enumerate(edge_ids):
        u=U[eid]; v=V[eid]; w=T[eid]
        starts[idx][0]=u; ends[idx][0]=v
        starts[idx][1]=v; ends[idx][1]=u
        base += w
    size=1<<K
    dp = [[[INF]*2 for _ in range(K)] for _ in range(size)]
    for i in range(K):
        for d in (0,1):
            dp[1<<i][i][d] = dist[0][starts[i][d]]
    for mask in range(1,size):
        # maybe skip if mask has one? Could start transitions from all masks
        # For each i in mask, d with finite dp
        for i in range(K):
            if not (mask>>i)&1: continue
            row_i = dp[mask][i]
            for d in range(2):
                cur = row_i[d]
                if cur >= INF: continue
                end_i = ends[i][d]
                # add j not in mask
                rem = full ^ mask? Actually remaining = ((1<<K)-1) ^ mask
                # iterate bits
                r = remaining
                while r:
                    lb = r & -r
                    j = bit_index[lb]? Need mapping. Could use for j in range(K) if not mask...
                    r -= lb
                ...
    answer = min(dp[full][i][d] + dist[ends[i][d]][N-1] for i,d)
    return base + answer

Now, let's verify lower bound more rigorously with first occurrences. Suppose optimal walk W. Let e_{π1},...,e_{πK} be order of first time each required edge is traversed. For each first traversal, choose orientation from endpoint a to b. Segment before first first-traversal contains no required edges (otherwise that would be first), so its cost >= dist(s,a_π1). Segment between first traversal of e_{πj} and e_{πj+1} contains no required edges that are not among e_{π1..πj}? Actually it may contain first traversal of e_{πj+1} at its end; before that, it contains no unvisited required edges. It may contain already visited required edges. Its cost >= dist(b_πj, a_π{j+1}) because any walk is a path (not necessarily simple) between those vertices; shortest path distance is lower bound. Segment after last contains no unvisited required edges (none left), may contain visited; cost >= dist(b_πK, t). Total cost = base (each required edge first traversal weight) + extra traversals + segment costs. Wait base sum includes weights of first traversals. Segment costs include weights of any edges traversed in segments, including maybe required edges already visited. The total cost of W = sum weights of all edge traversals. This equals sum of weights of first traversals (base) + weights of all other traversals (including segments and maybe repeated required edges). The segment costs as measured by actual walk include all traversals in segments, which include the first traversal of next edge? Need define segments carefully: Segment from after first traversal of e_j to before first traversal of e_{j+1} excludes the first traversal of e_{j+1} itself. Then total cost = base + sum actual segment costs. The actual segment costs >= dist connectors. So total >= base + sum dist connectors. Good. Thus optimum >= min_order (base+dist). Good.

Now, maybe there is a walk where first traversal of a required edge happens in a segment that starts after another required edge's first traversal, but the segment's shortest path lower bound uses a path that includes an unvisited required edge, which would change order. Lower bound still holds because dist is min over all paths, including those with unvisited edges. We don't need path avoid unvisited edges for lower bound. So fine.

Let's write code skeleton:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]; M = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
    U = [0]*M; V=[0]*M; T=[0]*M
    INF = 10**30
    dist = [[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    for e in range(M):
        u = data[idx]-1; v = data[idx+1]-1; t = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
        U[e]=u; V[e]=v; T[e]=t
        if t < dist[u][v]:
            dist[u][v] = t
            dist[v][u] = t
    # Floyd
    rng = range(N)
    for k in rng:
        dk = dist[k]
        for i in rng:
            dik = dist[i][k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            row = dist[i]
            # maybe local variables
            for j in rng:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < row[j]:
                    row[j] = nd
    Q = data[idx]; idx += 1
    out = []
    s = 0
    t = N-1
    # maybe precompute bit_count? no
    for _ in range(Q):
        K = data[idx]; idx += 1
        edge_ids = data[idx:idx+K]; idx += K
        # convert to 0-based? data are 1-based. We can subtract in loop.
        # build starts/ends
        starts0 = [0]*K; starts1 = [0]*K; ends0=[0]*K; ends1=[0]*K? Or arrays.
        base = 0
        for a, eid in enumerate(edge_ids):
            e = eid - 1
            u = U[e]; v=V[e]; w=T[e]
            # orientation 0: start u end v; orientation 1: start v end u
            # We can store start0[a]=u, end0[a]=v, start1[a]=v, end1[a]=u
            base += w
        # DP
        size = 1 << K
        # dp[mask][i][0/1]
        dp = [[[INF, INF] for _ in range(K)] for __ in range(size)]
        for i in range(K):
            # need edge endpoints; but we didn't store in arrays. Need store.
        ...
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Need store endpoints arrays. We can store `starts = [[0,0] for _ in range(K)]`, `ends = [[0,0] for _ in range(K)]` or four lists. K small, list of tuples fine.
For each edge:
u=U[e]; v=V[e]
starts[i][0]=u; starts[i][1]=v
ends[i][0]=v; ends[i][1]=u
base += T[e]

DP initialization:
for i in range(K):
    dp[1<<i][i][0] = dist[s][starts[i][0]]
    dp[1<<i][i][1] = dist[s][starts[i][1]]

Transition:
full = size - 1
for mask in range(1, size):
    # optional if mask == full skip transitions
    if mask == full: continue
    # for i in mask
    for i in range(K):
        if not (mask >> i) & 1: continue
        # for d in 0,1
        cur0 = dp[mask][i][0]
        cur1 = dp[mask][i][1]
        # for j not in mask
        for j in range(K):
            if (mask >> j) & 1: continue
            nmask = mask | (1 << j)
            # orientation e=0: start=starts[j][0], end=ends[j][0]
            # e=1
            # from i,d=0
            if cur0 < INF:
                end_i = ends[i][0]
                val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts[j][0]]
                if val < dp[nmask][j][0]: dp[nmask][j][0] = val
                val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts[j][1]]
                if val < dp[nmask][j][1]: dp[nmask][j][1] = val
            if cur1 < INF:
                end_i = ends[i][1]
                val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts[j][0]]
                if val < dp[nmask][j][0]: dp[nmask][j][0] = val
                val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts[j][1]]
                if val < dp[nmask][j][1]: dp[nmask][j][1] = val

But note if cur0 INF, still maybe cur1 finite. We can simplify:
for d in range(2):
    cur = dp[mask][i][d]
    if cur >= INF: continue
    end_i = ends[i][d]
    for j ...
        for e in range(2):
            val = cur + dist[end_i][starts[j][e]]
...
This is cleaner but slower due to range loops. K small, okay. But 3000 queries, 800 transitions, each with loops, maybe fine. We can optimize with unrolled for d/e? Not necessary but can.

At end:
best = INF
for i in range(K):
    for d in range(2):
        val = dp[full][i][d] + dist[ends[i][d]][t]
        if val < best: best = val
ans = base + best
out.append(str(ans))

Now, is there any need to consider traversing a required edge more than once as part of the "base" because maybe the optimal walk traverses a required edge in both directions and the first occurrence orientation plus extra not captured? Extra traversals are captured by connector distances if they occur between first occurrences. But what if the optimal walk traverses a required edge twice consecutively (back and forth) at the very beginning before any other required edge, and this somehow reduces total cost? Traversing back and forth adds cost, never reduces shortest path distances. It could change parity but we don't require Euler trail; any walk can have arbitrary extra cycles. No need for parity because we're not requiring an Euler trail of a multigraph; we're just finding shortest walk covering edges. The first-occurrence compression already shows extra traversals only add cost, so never beneficial except as part of shortest paths between first occurrences? Wait, extra traversals might be necessary to connect endpoints if the graph of required edges plus shortest paths? But our construction doesn't need parity because we explicitly order required edges and connect with shortest paths. Any sequence of required edge traversals connected by shortest paths is a valid walk; no parity constraints. So extra traversals are never needed except as connectors. Good.

Now, let's think if the problem can be solved by "Steiner tree" or "Chinese postman" but our approach is simpler due to K<=5. Need ensure no hidden issue: The required edges are bridges, and we must use each at least once. A walk can traverse a bridge in either direction. We model each required edge as a directed item. The sequence of first traversals is a permutation. Between first traversals, the actual walk may include other required edges already visited, but shortest path lower bound. The construction for a chosen order uses shortest paths between endpoints; if a shortest path includes a required edge that is scheduled later, then the actual first occurrence order changes. But the cost is still base+dist. However, if the shortest path includes a scheduled later required edge, then when we later traverse that required edge as an item, we are traversing it a second time. The total cost includes its weight twice (once in dist, once in base). Could there be a cheaper walk with the same order but using a different path that avoids the later required edge, with cost less than dist? No, dist is minimum; any path cost >= dist. If dist path includes later required edge, then the cost is dist. The constructed walk cost = base + dist. It is feasible. It might be not minimal for that order because maybe another path avoiding later required edge has cost slightly higher than dist but lower than dist + later item weight? Wait, in constructed walk, base includes later item weight regardless. If connector uses later required edge, then total includes its weight twice. If we instead choose a connector path that avoids later required edge, total would be base + connector_cost. If connector_cost > dist but < dist + w_later, then total could be lower than base + dist? But base + dist includes w_later once (base) plus dist (which includes w_later if using that edge). Actually if dist path uses later required edge, dist = w_later + other. Then total = base + w_later + other = (sum all base) + w_later + other. If avoid later edge, connector_cost = other' maybe > dist but not including w_later. Total = base + other'. Compare: base + dist = base + w_later + other. If other' < w_later + other, then avoiding is cheaper. But dist is minimum, so dist <= other'. Thus w_later + other <=? Wait dist <= other' because dist is shortest path between connector endpoints. If dist uses later required edge, dist = w_later + other. Since dist <= other', we have w_later + other <= other'. Thus base+dist <= base+other'. So using dist path is no more expensive than avoiding. But note if dist path uses later required edge, the later item is traversed twice; but base+dist <= base+other'. So construction is optimal for that order. Good.

But there is a subtlety: The lower bound for an optimal walk with first-occurrence order uses actual segment costs, which may avoid unvisited required edges. The connector distance dist may be achieved by a path that uses unvisited required edges. The lower bound says segment cost >= dist, so total >= base+dist for that order. Thus min_order <= optimal. Construction for min_order uses dist paths and may use unvisited edges, but cost = base+dist. Since base+dist <= optimal? Wait lower bound says optimal >= base+dist for its own order, so optimal >= min_order. Construction says min_order feasible, so optimal <= min_order. Thus equality. The construction cost is base+dist for the chosen order. Even if dist path uses later required edges, the walk is feasible and cost base+dist. It may have first occurrence order different, but that doesn't matter. So no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is a better walk that uses a required edge as part of a shortest path connector and does not separately traverse it as an item in the order, but still uses all required edges because some connectors collectively cover them. Our enumeration includes an order where that edge's first occurrence is in the connector? But our model requires each required edge to be an item with base cost and a distinct traversal. If a connector covers a required edge, then in the actual walk, that edge is traversed. The base cost should count that traversal, not an extra separate traversal. Our enumeration with item traversal separately would count it twice if connector also uses it. Could there be a walk where a required edge is traversed only as part of a connector between other required edges, and not as a separate item, yielding lower cost than any order+base+dist? Let's examine.

In our model, we take first occurrences of all required edges. If a required edge is traversed only as part of a connector between first occurrences of other required edges, then its first occurrence is within that connector. The order of first occurrences would place it between those other edges (or before/after). Our enumeration over all permutations includes that order. The segment between the first occurrence of the previous required edge and this edge's first occurrence is a subpath of the connector; its cost >= dist. The segment between this edge's first occurrence and the next required edge's first occurrence is the rest; cost >= dist. The base includes this edge's weight. The connector cost in our formula is sum of shortest distances between consecutive first occurrences, which may be less than or equal to actual connector subpaths. Thus total lower bound holds. Construction for that order uses shortest paths between first occurrences and traverses each item separately. It might traverse the edge as an item and also in a connector if shortest path uses it, but that's extra. The cost is base+dist. Could it be higher than the actual walk that used edge only in connector? The actual walk cost = base + actual connector subpath costs. Since actual connector subpath costs >= dist connectors, actual cost >= base+dist. So base+dist is <= actual. And base+dist is feasible (by traversing items separately), so optimum <= base+dist. Thus optimum <= base+dist <= actual? Wait if actual is optimal, actual = optimum. Then optimum >= base+dist and optimum <= base+dist, so equality. So fine. The construction may not be the same as actual but same cost.

Thus model correct.

Let's implement a fast integer parser that reads all bytes and yields ints lazily, without split. It stores the bytes string only (input size maybe <10 MB). Then we can build edges, run Floyd, then continue parsing queries from the same bytes. This avoids data int list and split bytes objects. Code:

import sys
data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
idx = 0
L = len(data)
def next_int():
    nonlocal? In main, can't use nonlocal at module. Use index variable in list? Or define a class? Simpler:
class FastScanner:
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)
    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + (data[i] - 48)
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num

This parses positive ints. All input positive except none. It stores data bytes. Method call for each int ~600k, okay. Could optimize by localizing `next_int = fs.next_int` but method call overhead. 600k calls okay. Actually M=2e5, edges 600k ints, queries 15k, total 618k calls. Fine.

def ints():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    num = 0
    in_num = False
    for c in data:
        if c >= 48:
            num = num*10 + c-48
            in_num = True
        else:
            if in_num:
                yield num
                num = 0
                in_num = False
    if in_num:
        yield num

Let's outline code with data list:

def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx=0
    N=data[idx]; M=data[idx+1]; idx+=2
    U=[0]*M; V=[0]*M; T=[0]*M
    INF=10**30
    dist=[[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N): dist[i][i]=0
    for e in range(M):
        u=data[idx]-1; v=data[idx+1]-1; t=data[idx+2]; idx+=3
        U[e]=u; V[e]=v; T[e]=t
        if t < dist[u][v]:
            dist[u][v]=t; dist[v][u]=t
    Q=data[idx]; idx+=1
    queries=[]
    for _ in range(Q):
        K=data[idx]; idx+=1
        queries.append(data[idx:idx+K])  # 1-based IDs
        idx += K
    del data
    # Floyd
    rng=range(N)
    for k in rng:
        dk=dist[k]
        for i in rng:
            dik=dist[i][k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            row=dist[i]
            # maybe local dk_j? 
            for j in rng:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < row[j]:
                    row[j] = nd
    s=0; t=N-1
    out=[]
    # maybe precompute dist_to_t = [dist[i][t] for i in range(N)]
    dist_to_t = [dist[i][t] for i in range(N)]
    for edge_ids in queries:
        K=len(edge_ids)
        starts0=[0]*K; starts1=[0]*K; ends0=[0]*K; ends1=[0]*K
        base=0
        for i,eid in enumerate(edge_ids):
            e=eid-1
            u=U[e]; v=V[e]; w=T[e]
            starts0[i]=u; starts1[i]=v
            ends0[i]=v; ends1[i]=u
            base += w
        size=1<<K
        dp = [[[INF, INF] for _ in range(K)] for __ in range(size)]
        # init
        for i in range(K):
            dp[1<<i][i][0] = dist[s][starts0[i]]
            dp[1<<i][i][1] = dist[s][starts1[i]]
        full=size-1
        for mask in range(1, size):
            if mask == full:
                break? Wait if mask==full, no transitions; but range includes full. We can loop `for mask in range(1, full):` to exclude full. But if K=1, full=1, range(1,1) empty. Good. Use `for mask in range(1, full):` because transitions only from non-full masks. But what about masks with no finite states? skip. Numeric order okay.
        for mask in range(1, full):
            for i in range(K):
                if not (mask >> i) & 1: continue
                cur0 = dp[mask][i][0]
                cur1 = dp[mask][i][1]
                # For each j not in mask
                for j in range(K):
                    if (mask >> j) & 1: continue
                    nmask = mask | (1 << j)
                    # from orientation 0
                    if cur0 < INF:
                        end_i = ends0[i]
                        val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nmask][j][0]: dp[nmask][j][0] = val
                        val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nmask][j][1]: dp[nmask][j][1] = val
                    if cur1 < INF:
                        end_i = ends1[i]
                        val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nmask][j][0]: dp[nmask][j][0] = val
                        val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nmask][j][1]: dp[nmask][j][1] = val
        best = INF
        for i in range(K):
            val = dp[full][i][0] + dist_to_t[ends0[i]]
            if val < best: best = val
            val = dp[full][i][1] + dist_to_t[ends1[i]]
            if val < best: best = val
        out.append(str(base + best))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Optimal walk: s -> ... traverse e2 as part of path from end of e1 to t? Actually if e2 is traversed only in connector after e1, then first occurrence order is e1 then e2. Our formula for order e1,e2: base w1+w2 + dist(s,start1)+dist(end1,start2)+dist(end2,t). The actual connector from end1 to t includes e2; its cost >= dist(end1,start2)+dist(end2,t)? The cost of actual segment = cost from end1 to start2 (before e2) + w2 + cost from end2 to t. This is >= dist(end1,start2) + w2 + dist(end2,t). Total actual = w1 + segment = w1 + w2 + dist... So lower bound. Good.

    dp[idx0 + 1] = dist_s[starts1[i]]

    # iterate i in mask
    # We can loop i in range(K) and check
    for i in range(K):
        if not (mask >> i) & 1:
            continue
        base_idx = (mask * K + i) * 2
        cur0 = dp[base_idx]
        cur1 = dp[base_idx + 1]

Custom scanner:
class FastScanner:
    __slots__ = ('data', 'idx', 'n')
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)
    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + data[i] - 48
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num

All numbers positive, so no sign. `data[i] > 32` includes digits only; no negative. Good. If at EOF, not called. This stores data bytes until scanner object freed. We can parse queries into lists and then maybe `fs.data = b''`? Not needed. But data bytes remains; memory maybe input size ~8MB. Fine. It avoids data int list and split bytes. Let's use this for memory and maybe speed? The Python-level byte loop may be slower than split, but input size small. 600k ints, each digit loop; maybe ~6M iterations, okay. `split` is C faster but memory. I think custom scanner acceptable.

However, method call `next_int` for each int 600k; each call loops over whitespace and digits. Could be slower than `map(int, split)` but still likely <1s? Python 6M byte iterations ~0.2-0.4s? Actually Python loop over bytes 6M ~0.3s? Maybe 0.5-1s. Fine.

But in PyPy, custom scanner can be slower. `split` is often faster. AtCoder PyPy with `map(int, read().split())` is common. Since memory okay, use split for speed. I'd use split.

Let's implement custom scanner efficiently:
import sys

class FastScanner:
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)
    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + (data[i] - 48)
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num

We can localize `next_int = fs.next_int` in main for speed. But method still uses self. Could define a closure with `idx` as list? Not necessary.

Now, let's write code with split:

def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]; M = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
    U = [0]*M
    V = [0]*M
    T = [0]*M
    INF = 10**30
    dist = [[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    for e in range(M):
        u = data[idx] - 1
        v = data[idx+1] - 1
        t = data[idx+2]
        idx += 3
        U[e] = u
        V[e] = v
        T[e] = t
        if t < dist[u][v]:
            dist[u][v] = t
            dist[v][u] = t
    Q = data[idx]; idx += 1
    queries = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        K = data[idx]; idx += 1
        queries.append([data[idx+i]-1 for i in range(K)])
        idx += K
    del data

    # Floyd
    rng = range(N)
    for k in rng:
        dk = dist[k]
        for i, row in enumerate(dist):
            dik = row[k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            # local variables for speed
            for j in rng:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < row[j]:
                    row[j] = nd

    s = 0
    t = N - 1
    dist_s = dist[s]
    dist_to_t = [dist[i][t] for i in range(N)]
    out = []
    for edge_ids in queries:
        K = len(edge_ids)
        if K == 0:
            out.append(str(dist_s[t]))
            continue
        starts0 = [0]*K
        starts1 = [0]*K
        ends0 = [0]*K
        ends1 = [0]*K
        base = 0
        for i, e in enumerate(edge_ids):
            u = U[e]; v = V[e]; w = T[e]
            starts0[i] = u
            starts1[i] = v
            ends0[i] = v
            ends1[i] = u
            base += w
        size = 1 << K
        full = size - 1
        dp = [INF] * (size * K * 2)
        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = ((1 << i) * K + i) * 2
            dp[idx0] = dist_s[starts0[i]]
            dp[idx0 + 1] = dist_s[starts1[i]]
        for mask in range(1, full):
            for i in range(K):
                if not (mask >> i) & 1:
                    continue
                base_idx = (mask * K + i) * 2
                cur0 = dp[base_idx]
                cur1 = dp[base_idx + 1]
                if cur0 >= INF and cur1 >= INF:
                    continue
                for j in range(K):
                    if (mask >> j) & 1:
                        continue
                    nmask = mask | (1 << j)
                    nidx = (nmask * K + j) * 2
                    if cur0 < INF:
                        end_i = ends0[i]
                        val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur0 + dist[end_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val
                    if cur1 < INF:
                        end_i = ends1[i]
                        val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur1 + dist[end_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val
        best = INF
        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = (full * K + i) * 2
            val = dp[idx0] + dist_to_t[ends0[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val
            val = dp[idx0 + 1] + dist_to_t[ends1[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val
        out.append(str(base + best))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Let's try to prove exactness one more time with a formal exchange:
Let R be required edges. For any walk W from s to t, let e1..eK be order of first traversals of edges in R. For each ej, let aj,bj be endpoints in traversal direction. Then W cost = sum w(ej) + sum costs of subwalks between these first traversals (excluding the first traversals themselves). Each subwalk from bj to a_{j+1} (with b0=s? Actually before e1 from s to a1, and after eK from bK to t) has cost at least dist between its endpoints. Therefore W cost >= sum w + dist(s,a1)+Σ dist(bj,a_{j+1})+dist(bK,t). This RHS is one of the values considered by DP (with order and orientations). So OPT >= DP_min? Wait DP_min is minimum over all orders/orientations of that RHS. Since for W's order, RHS >= DP_min? Actually DP_min <= RHS for that order. So W cost >= RHS >= DP_min. Thus OPT >= DP_min.

Conversely, for any order/orientation, construct a walk: start at s, take a shortest path to a1, traverse e1, take shortest path to a2, traverse e2, ..., traverse eK, take shortest path to t. This walk uses every required edge at least once (the explicit traversals), and has cost exactly sum w + sum dist connectors = DP value. Thus OPT <= DP_min. Therefore equality. This proof doesn't require connectors avoiding required edges. Good.

Now, let's think about if the constructed walk's explicit traversal of e_j after a connector that already traversed e_j means e_j is used at least once; yes. It may use some required edges before their explicit traversal, but still uses all. Good.

Now, let's think about if the DP_min value from construction could be lower than the actual cost of the constructed walk because shortest path distances might share edges? No, cost is sum of distances; following shortest paths sequentially and traversing items yields exactly sum. If a shortest path uses an edge that is also in another shortest path, we traverse it twice; cost sum counts twice. That's correct. If a shortest path uses a required edge, we traverse it in connector and later explicit; cost sum counts twice. Correct. So construction cost equals DP value. Good.

Let's write DP transition carefully:

for mask in range(1, full):
    for i in range(K):
        if not (mask >> i) & 1:
            continue
        base_idx = (mask * K + i) * 2
        cur0 = dp[base_idx]
        cur1 = dp[base_idx + 1]
        if cur0 >= INF and cur1 >= INF:
            continue
        end0_i = ends0[i]
        end1_i = ends1[i]
        for j in range(K):
            if (mask >> j) & 1:
                continue
            nmask = mask | (1 << j)
            nidx = (nmask * K + j) * 2
            if cur0 < INF:
                val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts0[j]]
                if val < dp[nidx]:
                    dp[nidx] = val
                val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts1[j]]
                if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                    dp[nidx + 1] = val
            if cur1 < INF:
                val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts0[j]]
                if val < dp[nidx]:
                    dp[nidx] = val
                val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts1[j]]
                if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                    dp[nidx + 1] = val

Note: If cur0 < INF and cur1 < INF, we update both. If one INF, skip. Good.

Could we combine if cur0/cur1 checks outside j? We already check both INF. If one INF, still loop j. Fine.

Implement:
class FastScanner:
    __slots__ = ("data", "idx", "n")
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)
    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + (data[i] - 48)
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num

Full code:

import sys

class FastScanner:
    __slots__ = ("data", "idx", "n")
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)
    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + (data[i] - 48)
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num

def main():
    fs = FastScanner()
    next_int = fs.next_int
    N = next_int()
    M = next_int()
    U = [0] * M
    V = [0] * M
    T = [0] * M
    INF = 10 ** 30
    dist = [[INF] * N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    for e in range(M):
        u = next_int() - 1
        v = next_int() - 1
        t = next_int()
        U[e] = u
        V[e] = v
        T[e] = t
        if t < dist[u][v]:
            dist[u][v] = t
            dist[v][u] = t
    Q = next_int()
    queries = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        K = next_int()
        q = [0] * K
        for i in range(K):
            q[i] = next_int() - 1
        queries.append(q)
    fs.data = b''  # release input buffer
    # Floyd-Warshall
    rng = range(N)
    for k in rng:
        dk = dist[k]
        for row in dist:
            dik = row[k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            for j in rng:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < row[j]:
                    row[j] = nd
    s = 0
    t = N - 1
    D = dist
    dist_s = D[s]
    dist_to_t = [D[i][t] for i in range(N)]
    out = []
    for edge_ids in queries:
        K = len(edge_ids)
        if K == 0:
            out.append(str(dist_s[t]))
            continue
        starts0 = [0] * K
        starts1 = [0] * K
        ends0 = [0] * K
        ends1 = [0] * K
        base = 0
        for i, e in enumerate(edge_ids):
            u = U[e]
            v = V[e]
            w = T[e]
            starts0[i] = u
            starts1[i] = v
            ends0[i] = v
            ends1[i] = u
            base += w
        size = 1 << K
        full = size - 1
        dp = [INF] * (size * K * 2)
        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = ((1 << i) * K + i) * 2
            dp[idx0] = dist_s[starts0[i]]
            dp[idx0 + 1] = dist_s[starts1[i]]
        for mask in range(1, full):
            for i in range(K):
                if ((mask >> i) & 1) == 0:
                    continue
                base_idx = (mask * K + i) * 2
                cur0 = dp[base_idx]
                cur1 = dp[base_idx + 1]
                if cur0 >= INF and cur1 >= INF:
                    continue
                end0_i = ends0[i]
                end1_i = ends1[i]
                for j in range(K):
                    if (mask >> j) & 1:
                        continue
                    nmask = mask | (1 << j)
                    nidx = (nmask * K + j) * 2
                    if cur0 < INF:
                        val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val
                    if cur1 < INF:
                        val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val
        best = INF
        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = (full * K + i) * 2
            val = dp[idx0] + dist_to_t[ends0[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val
            val = dp[idx0 + 1] + dist_to_t[ends1[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val
        out.append(str(base + best))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys


class FastScanner:
    __slots__ = ("data", "idx", "n")

    def __init__(self):
        self.data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
        self.idx = 0
        self.n = len(self.data)

    def next_int(self):
        data = self.data
        n = self.n
        i = self.idx
        while i < n and data[i] <= 32:
            i += 1
        num = 0
        while i < n and data[i] > 32:
            num = num * 10 + (data[i] - 48)
            i += 1
        self.idx = i
        return num


def main():
    fs = FastScanner()
    next_int = fs.next_int

    N = next_int()
    M = next_int()

    U = [0] * M
    V = [0] * M
    T = [0] * M

    INF = 10 ** 30
    dist = [[INF] * N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0

    for e in range(M):
        u = next_int() - 1
        v = next_int() - 1
        t = next_int()
        U[e] = u
        V[e] = v
        T[e] = t
        if t < dist[u][v]:
            dist[u][v] = t
            dist[v][u] = t

    Q = next_int()
    queries = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        K = next_int()
        q = [0] * K
        for i in range(K):
            q[i] = next_int() - 1
        queries.append(q)

    fs.data = b""

    # All-pairs shortest paths
    rng = range(N)
    for k in rng:
        dk = dist[k]
        for row in dist:
            dik = row[k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            for j in rng:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < row[j]:
                    row[j] = nd

    s = 0
    t = N - 1
    D = dist
    dist_s = D[s]
    dist_to_t = [D[i][t] for i in range(N)]

    out = []

    for edge_ids in queries:
        K = len(edge_ids)
        if K == 0:
            out.append(str(dist_s[t]))
            continue

        starts0 = [0] * K
        starts1 = [0] * K
        ends0 = [0] * K
        ends1 = [0] * K
        base = 0

        for i, e in enumerate(edge_ids):
            u = U[e]
            v = V[e]
            w = T[e]
            starts0[i] = u
            starts1[i] = v
            ends0[i] = v
            ends1[i] = u
            base += w

        size = 1 << K
        full = size - 1

        # dp[mask][last_item][orientation]
        dp = [INF] * (size * K * 2)

        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = ((1 << i) * K + i) * 2
            dp[idx0] = dist_s[starts0[i]]
            dp[idx0 + 1] = dist_s[starts1[i]]

        for mask in range(1, full):
            for i in range(K):
                if ((mask >> i) & 1) == 0:
                    continue

                base_idx = (mask * K + i) * 2
                cur0 = dp[base_idx]
                cur1 = dp[base_idx + 1]

                if cur0 >= INF and cur1 >= INF:
                    continue

                end0_i = ends0[i]
                end1_i = ends1[i]

                for j in range(K):
                    if (mask >> j) & 1:
                        continue

                    nmask = mask | (1 << j)
                    nidx = (nmask * K + j) * 2

                    if cur0 < INF:
                        val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur0 + D[end0_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val

                    if cur1 < INF:
                        val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts0[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx]:
                            dp[nidx] = val
                        val = cur1 + D[end1_i][starts1[j]]
                        if val < dp[nidx + 1]:
                            dp[nidx + 1] = val

        best = INF
        for i in range(K):
            idx0 = (full * K + i) * 2
            val = dp[idx0] + dist_to_t[ends0[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val
            val = dp[idx0 + 1] + dist_to_t[ends1[i]]
            if val < best:
                best = val

        out.append(str(base + best))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
